Like a payment number or something in a client proof, or even whether the door really latched. It makes me feel really on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to repeat certain actions, like checking, to make these doubts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ll reopen files, reread emails, check the online payment confirmation again… sometimes more than once. And with numbers, I’ll sort of repeat the figures silently in my head until they feel settled. It can make something simple, like an invoice or a logo revision, take hours that I can’t even bill for.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, beyond just these doubts about mistakes?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the same kind of thought, just repeating over and over. Like, I’ll know I checked the mortgage number or the client proof, but then it pops back in: what if I missed something important? What if this is the one mistake that causes a big problem? It’s hard to shake, even when part of me knows I already looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors beyond checking to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly it’s the checking, but there’s also this mental part where I’ll repeat the figures silently in my head until they feel right or settled. I’ll reopen files, reread emails, check the payment confirmation again… and it can turn something simple into hours of work I can’t really bill for.\n\nDoctor: Are these doubts and checking behaviors interfering with your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. Work is the biggest place I notice it. I’ve lost billable hours because I keep checking things, and deadlines get pushed back. Something that used to take me an afternoon can drag on for days now.\n\nAnd, honestly, one longtime client has already asked if I can still handle simple revisions on time, which was… embarrassing and upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that are not about making mistakes or checking, but that still feel intrusive or unwanted?\n\nPatient: Not really outside of that theme. It’s mostly these awful doubts that barge in, like maybe I missed a number on the mortgage payment, or a clause in a client proof, or even something like whether a door was latched. Even after I’ve checked, the thought comes back with this feeling of, what if this is the one mistake that costs me everything?\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress you feel when these doubts and the need to check things repeatedly occur?\n\nPatient: It gets pretty intense. I feel like I can’t relax until I’ve gone back in and checked again. I’ll reopen the file, reread the email, look at the payment confirmation, or repeat the numbers in my head until they feel settled. It’s upsetting because I know it should be simple, but it can eat up hours, and then I’m sitting there frustrated and anxious because I can’t bill for all that time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations that you avoid because of these doubts and the need to check things repeatedly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid specific tasks, exactly. I still do them, but I definitely hesitate more before starting things I know will involve a lot of details. I may put them off because I know I’m going to get stuck checking, but I don’t think there’s a whole category of things I completely avoid.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or tasks that you used to do without difficulty but now find yourself avoiding because of these doubts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything I flat-out stopped doing that I used to do easily. It’s more that I dread certain detailed things now, or I take longer to get myself to start them because I know I’ll probably get caught checking. But I’m still doing them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep getting these awful doubts that I’ve missed something important. Like a number on the mortgage payment, or a clause in a client proof, or whether a door actually latched. Even when I know I checked, the thought comes right back—like, what if this is the mistake that costs me everything?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these doubts occur?\n\nPatient: Yes. It gets pretty intense. The doubt just barges back in even after I’ve checked, and then I’m thinking, what if I missed the one thing that causes a huge problem? Like a payment number or something in a client proof, or even whether the door really latched. It makes me feel really on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to repeat certain actions, like checking, to make these doubts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ll reopen files, reread emails, check the online payment confirmation again… sometimes more than once. And with numbers, I’ll sort of repeat the figures silently in my head until they feel settled. It can make something simple, like an invoice or a logo revision, take hours that I can’t even bill for.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, beyond just these doubts about mistakes?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the same kind of thought, just repeating over and over. Like, I’ll know I checked the mortgage number or the client proof, but then it pops back in: what if I missed something important? What if this is the one mistake that causes a big problem? It’s hard to shake, even when part of me knows I already looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors beyond checking to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly it’s the checking, but there’s also this mental part where I’ll repeat the figures silently in my head until they feel right or settled. I’ll reopen files, reread emails, check the payment confirmation again… and it can turn something simple into hours of work I can’t really bill for.\n\nDoctor: Are these doubts and checking behaviors interfering with your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. Work is the biggest place I notice it. I’ve lost billable hours because I keep checking things, and deadlines get pushed back. Even after I’ve checked, the thought comes back with this feeling of, what if this is the one mistake that costs me everything?\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress you feel when these doubts and the need to check things repeatedly occur?\n\nPatient: It gets pretty intense. I feel like I can’t relax until I’ve gone back in and checked again. It’s upsetting because I know it should be simple, but it can eat up hours, and then I’m sitting there frustrated and anxious because I can’t bill for all that time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations that you avoid because of these doubts and the need to check things repeatedly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid specific tasks, exactly. I still do them, but I definitely hesitate more before starting things I know will involve a lot of details. I may put them off because I know I’m going to get stuck checking, but I don’t think there’s a whole category of things I completely avoid.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or tasks that you used to do without difficulty but now find yourself avoiding because of these doubts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything I flat-out stopped doing that I used to do easily. It’s more that I dread certain detailed things now, or I take longer to get myself to start them because I know I’ll probably get caught checking. But I’m still doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your personal relationships due to these doubts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Not really, not in a major way. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep getting these awful doubts that I’ve missed something important. Like a number on the mortgage payment, or a clause in a client proof, or whether a door actually latched. Even when I know I checked, the thought comes right back—like, what if this is the mistake that costs me everything?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these doubts occur?\n\nPatient: Yes. It gets pretty intense. The doubt just barges back in even after I’ve checked, and then I’m thinking, what if I missed the one thing that causes a huge problem? Like a payment number or something in a client proof, or even whether the door really latched. It makes me feel really on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to repeat certain actions, like checking, to make these doubts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ll reopen files, reread emails, check the online payment confirmation again… sometimes more than once. And with numbers, I’ll sort of repeat the figures silently in my head until they feel settled. It can make something simple, like an invoice or a logo revision, take hours that I can’t even bill for.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, beyond just these doubts about mistakes?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the same kind of thought, just repeating over and over. Like, I’ll know I checked the mortgage number or the client proof, but then it pops back in: what if I missed something important? What if this is the one mistake that causes a big problem? It’s hard to shake, even when part of me knows I already looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors beyond checking to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly it’s the checking, but there’s also this mental part where I’ll repeat the figures silently in my head until they feel right or settled. I’ll reopen files, reread emails, check the payment confirmation again… and it can turn something simple into hours of work I can’t really bill for.\n\nDoctor: Are these doubts and checking behaviors interfering with your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. Work is the biggest place I notice it. I’ve lost billable hours because I keep checking things, and deadlines get pushed back. Something that used to take me an afternoon can drag on for days now.\n\nAnd, honestly, one longtime client has already asked if I can still handle simple revisions on time, which was… embarrassing and upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that are not about making mistakes or checking, but that still feel intrusive or unwanted?\n\nPatient: Not really outside of that theme. It’s mostly these awful doubts that barge in, like maybe I missed a number on the mortgage payment, or a clause in a client proof, or even something like whether a door was latched. Even after I’ve checked, the thought comes back with this feeling of, what if this is the one mistake that costs me everything?\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress you feel when these doubts and the need to check things repeatedly occur?\n\nPatient: It gets pretty intense. I feel like I can’t relax until I’ve gone back in and checked again. I’ll reopen the file, reread the email, look at the payment confirmation, or repeat the numbers in my head until they feel settled. It’s upsetting because I know it should be simple, but it can eat up hours, and then I’m sitting there frustrated and anxious because I can’t bill for all that time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations that you avoid because of these doubts and the need to check things repeatedly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid specific tasks, exactly. I still do them, but I definitely hesitate more before starting things I know will involve a lot of details. I may put them off because I know I’m going to get stuck checking, but I don’t think there’s a whole category of things I completely avoid.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or tasks that you used to do without difficulty but now find yourself avoiding because of these doubts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything I flat-out stopped doing that I used to do easily. It’s more that I dread certain detailed things now, or I take longer to get myself to start them because I know I’ll probably get caught checking. But I’m still doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your personal relationships due to these doubts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Not really, not in a major way. I don’t think it’s caused conflicts or anything. I might be more distracted or irritated when I’m stuck checking something, but I haven’t noticed it seriously affecting my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like these doubts and checking behaviors are affecting your overall well-being or daily life outside of your work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Outside of work, I don’t think it’s hugely affecting my daily life. It can make me feel tense or worn out sometimes when I get caught up in checking, but I’m still doing my usual things. I haven’t noticed a major change beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed by these doubts and checking behaviors to the point where you're considering seeking help to manage them, outside of the impact on your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think that’s actually why I’m here. It’s not just work exactly—it’s the doubts themselves. I keep getting this awful feeling that I’ve missed something important, like a number on a mortgage payment, or a clause in a client proof, or even whether a door really latched. And even after I check, the thought comes back, like, what if this is the mistake that costs me everything? So yes, I’d like help managing that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Like a payment number or something in a client proof, or even whether the door really latched. It makes me feel really on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to repeat certain actions, like checking, to make these doubts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ll reopen files, reread emails, check the online payment confirmation again… sometimes more than once. And with numbers, I’ll sort of repeat the figures silently in my head until they feel settled. It can make something simple, like an invoice or a logo revision, take hours that I can’t even bill for.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, beyond just these doubts about mistakes?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the same kind of thought, just repeating over and over. Like, I’ll know I checked the mortgage number or the client proof, but then it pops back in: what if I missed something important? What if this is the one mistake that causes a big problem? It’s hard to shake, even when part of me knows I already looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors beyond checking to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly it’s the checking, but there’s also this mental part where I’ll repeat the figures silently in my head until they feel right or settled. I’ll reopen files, reread emails, check the payment confirmation again… and it can turn something simple into hours of work I can’t really bill for.\n\nDoctor: Are these doubts and checking behaviors interfering with your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. Work is the biggest place I notice it. I’ve lost billable hours because I keep checking things, and deadlines get pushed back. Something that used to take me an afternoon can drag on for days now.\n\nAnd, honestly, one longtime client has already asked if I can still handle simple revisions on time, which was… embarrassing and upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that are not about making mistakes or checking, but that still feel intrusive or unwanted?\n\nPatient: Not really outside of that theme. It’s mostly these awful doubts that barge in, like maybe I missed a number on the mortgage payment, or a clause in a client proof, or even something like whether a door was latched. Even after I’ve checked, the thought comes back with this feeling of, what if this is the one mistake that costs me everything?\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress you feel when these doubts and the need to check things repeatedly occur?\n\nPatient: It gets pretty intense. I feel like I can’t relax until I’ve gone back in and checked again. I’ll reopen the file, reread the email, look at the payment confirmation, or repeat the numbers in my head until they feel settled. It’s upsetting because I know it should be simple, but it can eat up hours, and then I’m sitting there frustrated and anxious because I can’t bill for all that time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations that you avoid because of these doubts and the need to check things repeatedly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid specific tasks, exactly. I still do them, but I definitely hesitate more before starting things I know will involve a lot of details. I may put them off because I know I’m going to get stuck checking, but I don’t think there’s a whole category of things I completely avoid.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or tasks that you used to do without difficulty but now find yourself avoiding because of these doubts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything I flat-out stopped doing that I used to do easily. It’s more that I dread certain detailed things now, or I take longer to get myself to start them because I know I’ll probably get caught checking. But I’m still doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your personal relationships due to these doubts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Not really, not in a major way. I don’t think it’s caused conflicts or anything. I might be more distracted or irritated when I’m stuck checking something, but I haven’t noticed it seriously affecting my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like these doubts and checking behaviors are affecting your overall well-being or daily life outside of your work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Outside of work, I don’t think it’s hugely affecting my daily life. It can make me feel tense or worn out sometimes when I get caught up in checking, but I’m still doing my usual things. I haven’t noticed a major change beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed by these doubts and checking behaviors to the point where you're considering seeking help to manage them, outside of the impact on your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think that’s actually why I’m here. It’s not just work exactly—it’s the doubts themselves. I keep getting this awful feeling that I’ve missed something important, like a number on a mortgage payment, or a clause in a client proof, or even whether a door really latched. And even after I check, the thought comes back, like, what if this is the mistake that costs me everything? So yes, I’d like help managing that.\n\nDoctor: Are these doubts and checking behaviors affecting your ability to enjoy or participate in hobbies or leisure activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s really stopping me from doing hobbies or relaxing. I still do the things I usually do. Sometimes I’m a bit preoccupied, so I may not enjoy myself as much in the moment, but I wouldn’t say it’s kept me from participating.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "Determine if the patient's symptoms are causing significant distress or impairment in social or occupational functioning beyond what has already been described", "question": "Are there any other areas of your life where you feel like these doubts and checking behaviors are holding you back or causing problems?"}

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep getting these awful doubts that I’ve missed something important. Like a number on the mortgage payment, or a clause in a client proof, or whether a door actually latched. Even when I know I checked, the thought comes right back—like, what if this is the mistake that costs me everything?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these doubts occur?\n\nPatient: Yes. It gets pretty intense. The doubt just barges back in even after I’ve checked, and then I’m thinking, what if I missed the one thing that causes a huge problem? Like a payment number or something in a client proof, or even whether the door really latched. It makes me feel really on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to repeat certain actions, like checking, to make these doubts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ll reopen files, reread emails, check the online payment confirmation again… sometimes more than once. And with numbers, I’ll sort of repeat the figures silently in my head until they feel settled. It can make something simple, like an invoice or a logo revision, take hours that I can’t even bill for.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, beyond just these doubts about mistakes?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the same kind of thought, just repeating over and over. Like, I’ll know I checked the mortgage number or the client proof, but then it pops back in: what if I missed something important? What if this is the one mistake that causes a big problem? It’s hard to shake, even when part of me knows I already looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors beyond checking to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly it’s the checking, but there’s also this mental part where I’ll repeat the figures silently in my head until they feel right or settled. I’ll reopen files, reread emails, check the payment confirmation again… and it can turn something simple into hours of work I can’t really bill for.\n\nDoctor: Are these doubts and checking behaviors interfering with your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. Work is the biggest place I notice it. I’ve lost billable hours because I keep checking things, and deadlines get pushed back. Something that used to take me an afternoon can drag on for days now.\n\nAnd, honestly, one longtime client has already asked if I can still handle simple revisions on time, which was… embarrassing and upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that are not about making mistakes or checking, but that still feel intrusive or unwanted?\n\nPatient: Not really outside of that theme. It’s mostly these awful doubts that barge in, like maybe I missed a number on the mortgage payment, or a clause in a client proof, or even something like whether a door was latched. Even after I’ve checked, the thought comes back with this feeling of, what if this is the one mistake that costs me everything?\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress you feel when these doubts and the need to check things repeatedly occur?\n\nPatient: It gets pretty intense. I feel like I can’t relax until I’ve gone back in and checked again. I’ll reopen the file, reread the email, look at the payment confirmation, or repeat the numbers in my head until they feel settled. It’s upsetting because I know it should be simple, but it can eat up hours, and then I’m sitting there frustrated and anxious because I can’t bill for all that time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations that you avoid because of these doubts and the need to check things repeatedly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid specific tasks, exactly. I still do them, but I definitely hesitate more before starting things I know will involve a lot of details. I may put them off because I know I’m going to get stuck checking, but I don’t think there’s a whole category of things I completely avoid.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or tasks that you used to do without difficulty but now find yourself avoiding because of these doubts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything I flat-out stopped doing that I used to do easily. It’s more that I dread certain detailed things now, or I take longer to get myself to start them because I know I’ll probably get caught checking. But I’m still doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your personal relationships due to these doubts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Not really, not in a major way. I don’t think it’s caused conflicts or anything. I might be more distracted or irritated when I’m stuck checking something, but I haven’t noticed it seriously affecting my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like these doubts and checking behaviors are affecting your overall well-being or daily life outside of your work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Outside of work, I don’t think it’s hugely affecting my daily life. It can make me feel tense or worn out sometimes when I get caught up in checking, but I’m still doing my usual things. I haven’t noticed a major change beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed by these doubts and checking behaviors to the point where you're considering seeking help to manage them, outside of the impact on your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think that’s actually why I’m here. It’s not just work exactly—it’s the doubts themselves. I keep getting this awful feeling that I’ve missed something important, like a number on a mortgage payment, or a clause in a client proof, or even whether a door really latched. And even after I check, the thought comes back, like, what if this is the mistake that costs me everything? So yes, I’d like help managing that.\n\nDoctor: Are these doubts and checking behaviors affecting your ability to enjoy or participate in hobbies or leisure activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s really stopping me from doing hobbies or relaxing. I still do the things I usually do. Sometimes I’m a bit preoccupied, so I may not enjoy myself as much in the moment, but I wouldn’t say it’s kept me from participating.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life where you feel like these doubts and checking behaviors are holding you back or causing problems?\n\nPatient: Yes, mainly with work. I’ve missed billable hours because I keep rechecking things, and deadlines get pushed back. Work that used to take me an afternoon can drag on for days now. One longtime client even asked if I could still handle simple revisions on time, which was pretty upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations where you feel like your doubts and checking behaviors are completely uncontrollable?\n\nPatient: Yes, there are times it feels like I can’t stop. If I’m working on an invoice or a logo revision, I’ll reopen the file again and again, reread the email, check the online payment confirmation, and sometimes repeat the figures silently in my head until they feel right or “settled.” I know it sounds excessive, but in the moment it feels like I have to keep going, and something simple can turn into hours that I can’t even bill for.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep getting these awful doubts that I’ve missed something important. Like a number on the mortgage payment, or a clause in a client proof, or whether a door actually latched. Even when I know I checked, the thought comes right back—like, what if this is the mistake that costs me everything?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these doubts occur?\n\nPatient: Yes. It gets pretty intense. The doubt just barges back in even after I’ve checked, and then I’m thinking, what if I missed the one thing that causes a huge problem? It makes me feel really on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to repeat certain actions, like checking, to make these doubts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ll reopen files, reread emails, check the online payment confirmation again… sometimes more than once. And with numbers, I’ll sort of repeat the figures silently in my head until they feel settled. It can make something simple, like an invoice or a logo revision, take hours that I can’t even bill for.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, beyond just these doubts about mistakes?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the same kind of thought, just repeating over and over. Like, I’ll know I checked the mortgage number or the client proof, but then it pops back in: what if I missed something important? What if this is the one mistake that causes a big problem? It’s hard to shake, even when part of me knows I already looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors beyond checking to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly it’s the checking, but there’s also this mental part where I’ll repeat the figures silently in my head until they feel right or settled. I’ll reopen files, reread emails, check the payment confirmation again… and it can turn something simple into hours of work I can’t really bill for.\n\nDoctor: Are these doubts and checking behaviors interfering with your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. Work is the biggest place I notice it. I’ve lost billable hours because I keep checking things, and deadlines get pushed back. Something that used to take me an afternoon can drag on for days now.\n\nAnd, honestly, one longtime client has already asked if I can still handle simple revisions on time, which was… embarrassing and upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that are not about making mistakes or checking, but that still feel intrusive or unwanted?\n\nPatient: Not really outside of that theme. It’s mostly these awful doubts that barge in, like maybe I missed a number on the mortgage payment, or a clause in a client proof, or even something like whether a door was latched. Even after I’ve checked, the thought comes back with this feeling of, what if this is the one mistake that costs me everything?\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress you feel when these doubts and the need to check things repeatedly occur?\n\nPatient: It gets pretty intense. I feel like I can’t relax until I’ve gone back in and checked again. I’ll reopen the file, reread the email, look at the payment confirmation, or repeat the numbers in my head until they feel settled. It’s upsetting because I know it should be simple, but it can eat up hours, and then I’m sitting there frustrated and anxious because I can’t bill for all that time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations that you avoid because of these doubts and the need to check things repeatedly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid specific tasks, exactly. I still do them, but I definitely hesitate more before starting things I know will involve a lot of details. I may put them off because I know I’m going to get stuck checking, but I don’t think there’s a whole category of things I completely avoid.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or tasks that you used to do without difficulty but now find yourself avoiding because of these doubts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything I flat-out stopped doing that I used to do easily. It’s more that I dread certain detailed things now, or I take longer to get myself to start them because I know I’ll probably get caught checking. But I’m still doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your personal relationships due to these doubts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Not really, not in a major way. I don’t think it’s caused conflicts or anything. And even after I check, the thought comes back, like, what if this is the mistake that costs me everything? I still do the things I usually do. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep getting these awful doubts that I’ve missed something important. Like a number on the mortgage payment, or a clause in a client proof, or whether a door actually latched. Even when I know I checked, the thought comes right back—like, what if this is the mistake that costs me everything?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these doubts occur?\n\nPatient: Yes. It gets pretty intense. The doubt just barges back in even after I’ve checked, and then I’m thinking, what if I missed the one thing that causes a huge problem? Like a payment number or something in a client proof, or even whether the door really latched. It makes me feel really on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to repeat certain actions, like checking, to make these doubts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ll reopen files, reread emails, check the online payment confirmation again… sometimes more than once. And with numbers, I’ll sort of repeat the figures silently in my head until they feel settled. It can make something simple, like an invoice or a logo revision, take hours that I can’t even bill for.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, beyond just these doubts about mistakes?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the same kind of thought, just repeating over and over. Like, I’ll know I checked the mortgage number or the client proof, but then it pops back in: what if I missed something important? What if this is the one mistake that causes a big problem? It’s hard to shake, even when part of me knows I already looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors beyond checking to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly it’s the checking, but there’s also this mental part where I’ll repeat the figures silently in my head until they feel right or settled. I’ll reopen files, reread emails, check the payment confirmation again… and it can turn something simple into hours of work I can’t really bill for.\n\nDoctor: Are these doubts and checking behaviors interfering with your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. Work is the biggest place I notice it. I’ve lost billable hours because I keep checking things, and deadlines get pushed back. Something that used to take me an afternoon can drag on for days now.\n\nAnd, honestly, one longtime client has already asked if I can still handle simple revisions on time, which was… embarrassing and upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that are not about making mistakes or checking, but that still feel intrusive or unwanted?\n\nPatient: Not really outside of that theme. It’s mostly these awful doubts that barge in, like maybe I missed a number on the mortgage payment, or a clause in a client proof, or even something like whether a door was latched. Even after I’ve checked, the thought comes back with this feeling of, what if this is the one mistake that costs me everything?\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress you feel when these doubts and the need to check things repeatedly occur?\n\nPatient: It gets pretty intense. I feel like I can’t relax until I’ve gone back in and checked again. I’ll reopen the file, reread the email, look at the payment confirmation, or repeat the numbers in my head until they feel settled. It’s upsetting because I know it should be simple, but it can eat up hours, and then I’m sitting there frustrated and anxious because I can’t bill for all that time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations that you avoid because of these doubts and the need to check things repeatedly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid specific tasks, exactly. I still do them, but I definitely hesitate more before starting things I know will involve a lot of details. I may put them off because I know I’m going to get stuck checking, but I don’t think there’s a whole category of things I completely avoid.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or tasks that you used to do without difficulty but now find yourself avoiding because of these doubts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything I flat-out stopped doing that I used to do easily. It’s more that I dread certain detailed things now, or I take longer to get myself to start them because I know I’ll probably get caught checking. But I’m still doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your personal relationships due to these doubts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Not really, not in a major way. I don’t think it’s caused conflicts or anything. I might be more distracted or irritated when I’m stuck checking something, but I haven’t noticed it seriously affecting my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like these doubts and checking behaviors are affecting your overall well-being or daily life outside of your work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Outside of work, I don’t think it’s hugely affecting my daily life. It can make me feel tense or worn out sometimes when I get caught up in checking, but I’m still doing my usual things. I haven’t noticed a major change beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed by these doubts and checking behaviors to the point where you're considering seeking help to manage them, outside of the impact on your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think that’s actually why I’m here. It’s not just work exactly—it’s the doubts themselves. I keep getting this awful feeling that I’ve missed something important, like a number on a mortgage payment, or a clause in a client proof, or even whether a door really latched. And even after I check, the thought comes back, like, what if this is the mistake that costs me everything? So yes, I’d like help managing that.\n\nDoctor: Are these doubts and checking behaviors affecting your ability to enjoy or participate in hobbies or leisure activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s really stopping me from doing hobbies or relaxing. I still do the things I usually do. Sometimes I’m a bit preoccupied, so I may not enjoy myself as much in the moment, but I wouldn’t say it’s kept me from participating.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life where you feel like these doubts and checking behaviors are holding you back or causing problems?\n\nPatient: Yes, mainly with work. I’ve missed billable hours because I keep rechecking things, and deadlines get pushed back. Work that used to take me an afternoon can drag on for days now. One longtime client even asked if I could still handle simple revisions on time, which was pretty upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations where you feel like your doubts and checking behaviors are completely uncontrollable?\n\nPatient: Yes, there are times it feels like I can’t stop. If I’m working on an invoice or a logo revision, I’ll reopen the file again and again, reread the email, check the online payment confirmation, and sometimes repeat the figures silently in my head until they feel right or “settled.” I know it sounds excessive, but in the moment it feels like I have to keep going, and something simple can turn into hours that I can’t even bill for.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like these doubts and checking behaviors are significantly affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. Mostly because of work. I’m losing billable hours and pushing deadlines back because things that used to take me an afternoon can stretch into days. And having a longtime client question whether I can still handle simple revisions on time… that really made it feel like it’s affecting my life, not just being a little habit.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work, where you feel like these doubts and checking behaviors are causing you significant distress or problems?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, it’s stressful in general, but the main place I notice it causing real problems is work. Outside of that, I can’t think of another area where it’s been significant.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s upsetting because I know it should be simple, but it can eat up hours, and then I’m sitting there frustrated and anxious because I can’t bill for all that time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations that you avoid because of these doubts and the need to check things repeatedly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid specific tasks, exactly. I may put them off because I know I’m going to get stuck checking, but I don’t think there’s a whole category of things I completely avoid.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or tasks that you used to do without difficulty but now find yourself avoiding because of these doubts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything I flat-out stopped doing that I used to do easily. It’s more that I dread certain detailed things now, or I take longer to get myself to start them because I know I’ll probably get caught checking. But I’m still doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your personal relationships due to these doubts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Not really, not in a major way. I don’t think it’s caused conflicts or anything. I might be more distracted or irritated when I’m stuck checking something, but I haven’t noticed it seriously affecting my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like these doubts and checking behaviors are affecting your overall well-being or daily life outside of your work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Outside of work, I don’t think it’s hugely affecting my daily life. It can make me feel tense or worn out sometimes when I get caught up in checking, but I’m still doing my usual things. I haven’t noticed a major change beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed by these doubts and checking behaviors to the point where you're considering seeking help to manage them, outside of the impact on your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think that’s actually why I’m here. It’s not just work exactly—it’s the doubts themselves. I keep getting this awful feeling that I’ve missed something important, like a number on a mortgage payment, or a clause in a client proof, or even whether a door really latched. And even after I check, the thought comes back, like, what if this is the mistake that costs me everything? So yes, I’d like help managing that.\n\nDoctor: Are these doubts and checking behaviors affecting your ability to enjoy or participate in hobbies or leisure activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s really stopping me from doing hobbies or relaxing. I still do the things I usually do. Sometimes I’m a bit preoccupied, so I may not enjoy myself as much in the moment, but I wouldn’t say it’s kept me from participating.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life where you feel like these doubts and checking behaviors are holding you back or causing problems?\n\nPatient: Yes, mainly with work. I’ve missed billable hours because I keep rechecking things, and deadlines get pushed back. Work that used to take me an afternoon can drag on for days now. One longtime client even asked if I could still handle simple revisions on time, which was pretty upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations where you feel like your doubts and checking behaviors are completely uncontrollable?\n\nPatient: Yes, there are times it feels like I can’t stop. If I’m working on an invoice or a logo revision, I’ll reopen the file again and again, reread the email, check the online payment confirmation, and sometimes repeat the figures silently in my head until they feel right or “settled.” I know it sounds excessive, but in the moment it feels like I have to keep going, and something simple can turn into hours that I can’t even bill for.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like these doubts and checking behaviors are significantly affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. Mostly because of work. I’m losing billable hours and pushing deadlines back because things that used to take me an afternoon can stretch into days. And having a longtime client question whether I can still handle simple revisions on time… that really made it feel like it’s affecting my life, not just being a little habit.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work, where you feel like these doubts and checking behaviors are causing you significant distress or problems?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, it’s stressful in general, but the main place I notice it causing real problems is work. Outside of that, I can’t think of another area where it’s been significant.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these intrusive thoughts and checking behaviors are aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It’s like I’m trying to prevent a mistake from slipping through—like sending the wrong invoice amount or missing something in a revision. So I’ll reopen the file, reread the email, check the payment confirmation, and sometimes repeat the numbers in my head until they feel “settled.” It can take hours, even for something simple.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
